A reason for the absence of the author’s name in so many MSS. is found in the desire of à Kempis, if he indeed be the author, to remain incognito, in accordance with his own motto, ama nesciri, "love to be unknown."

Of the Latin editions belonging to the fifteenth century, Pohl gives 28 as accredited to Gerson, 12 to Thomas, 2 to St. Bernard, and 6 as anonymous. Or, to follow Funk, p. 426, 40 editions of that century were ascribed to Gerson, 11 to à Kempis, 2 to Bernard, 1 to Gersen, and 2 are anonymous. Spitzen gives 16 as ascribed to à Kempis. Most of the editions ascribing the work to Gerson were printed in France, the remaining editions being printed in Italy or Spain. The editions of the sixteenth century show a change, 37 Latin editions ascribing the authorship to à Kempis, and 25 to Gerson. As for the MSS. dated before 1460, and whose dates may be said to be reasonably above suspicion, all were written in Germany and the Lowlands. The oldest, included in a codex preserved since 1826 in the royal library of Brussels, probably belongs before 1420. The codex contains 9 other writings of à Kempis besides the Imitation, and contains the note, Finitus et completus MCCCCXLI per manus fratris Th. Kempensis in Monte S. Agnetis prope Zwollis (finished and completed, 1441, by the hands of brother Thomas à Kempis of Mount St. Agnes, near Zwolle). See Pohl, II. 461 sqq. So this is an autographic copy. The text of the Imitation, however, is written on older paper than the other documents, and has corrections which are found in a Dutch translation of the first book, dating from 1420. For these reasons, Funk, p. 424, and others, puts the MS. back to 1416–1420.

The literary controversy over the authorship began in 1604, when Dom Pedro Manriquez, in a work on the Lord’s Supper issued at Milan, and on the alleged basis of a quotation by Bonaventura, declared the Imitation to be older than that Schoolman. In 1606, Bellarmin, in his Descript. eccles., was more precise, and stated it was already in existence in 1260. About the same time, the Jesuit, Rossignoli, found in a convent at Arona, near Milan, a MS. without date, but bearing the name of an abbot, John Gersen, as its author; the house had belonged to the Benedictines once. In 1614 the Benedictine, Constantius Cajetan, secretary of Paul V., issued his Gersen restitutus at Rome, and later his Apparatus ad Gersenem restitutum, in which he defended the Italian’s claim. This individual was said to have been a Benedectine abbot of Vercelli, in Piedmont, in the first half of the thirteenth century. On the other hand, the Augustinian, Rosweyde, in his vindiciae Kempenses, Antwerp, 1617, so cogently defended the claims of à Kempis that Bellarmin withdrew his statement. In the nineteenth century the claims of Gersen were again urged by a Piedmontese nobleman, Gregory, in his Istoria della Vercellese letteratura, Turin, 1819, and subsequent publications, and by Wolfsgruber of Vienna in a scholarly work, 1880. But Hirsche and Funk are, no doubt, right in pronouncing the name Gersen a mistake for Gerson, and Funk, after careful criticism, declares the Italian abbot a fictitious personage. The most recent Engl. writer on the subject, Montmorency, p. xiii. says, "there is no evidence that there was ever an abbot of Vercelli by the name of Gersen."

The claims of John Gerson are of a substantial character, and France was not slow in coming to the chancellor’s defence. An examination of old MSS., made in Paris, had an uncertain issue, so that, in 1640, Richelieu’s splendid edition of the Imitation was sent forth without an author’s name. The French parliament, however, in 1652, ordered the book printed under the name of à Kempis. The matter was not settled and, at three gatherings, 1671, 1674, 1687, instituted by Mabillon, a fresh examination of MSS. was made, with the result that the case went against à Kempis. Later, Du Pin, after a comparison of Gerson’s writings with the Imitation, concluded that it was impossible to decide with certainty between these two writers and Gersen. (See his 2d ed. of Gerson’s Works, 1728, I. lix-lxxxiv) but in a special work. Amsterdam, 1706, he had decided in favor of the Dutchman. French editions of the Imitation continued to be issued under the name of Gerson, as, for example, those of Erhard-Mezler, 1724, and Vollardt, 1758. On the other hand, the Augustinian, Amort, defended the à Kempis authorship in his Informatio de statu controversiae, Augsburg, 1728, and especially in his Scutum Kempense, Cologne, 1728. After the unfavorable statement of Schwab, Life of Gerson, 1858, pp. 782–786, declaring that the Imitation is in an altogether different style from Gerson’s works, the theory of the Gerson authorship seemed to be finally abandoned. The first collected edition of Gerson’s Works, 1483, knows nothing about the Imitation. Nor did Gerson’s brother, prior of Lyons, mention it in the list he gave of the chancellor’s works, 1423. The author of the Imitation was, by his own statements, a monk, IV. 5, 11; III., 56. Gerson would have been obliged to change his usual habit of presentation to have written in the monastic tone.

After the question of authorship seemed to be pretty well settled in favor of à Kempis, another stage in the controversy was opened by the publications of Puyol in 1898, 1899. Puyol gives a description of 548 manuscripts, and makes a sharp distinction between those of Italian origin and other manuscripts. He also annotates the variations in 57, with the conclusion that the Italian text is the more simple, and consequently the older and original text. He himself based his edition on the text of Arona. Puyol is followed by Kentenich, and has been answered by Pohl and others.

Walter Hylton’s reputed authorship of the Imitation is based upon three books of that work, having gone under the name De musica ecclesiastica in MSS. in England and the persistent English tradition that Hylton was the author. Montmorency, pp. xiv, 138–170, while he pronounces the Hylton theory of authorship untenable, confesses his inability to explain it.

The arguments in favor of the à Kempis authorship, briefly stated, are as follows:—

  1. External testimony. John Busch, in his Chronicon Windesemense, written 1464, seven years before à Kempis’ death, expressly states that à Kempis wrote the Imitation. To this testimony are to be added the testimonies of Caspar of Pforzheim, who made a German translation of the work, 1448; Hermann Rheyd, who met Thomas, 1454, and John Wessel, who was attracted to Windesheim by the book’s fame. For other testimonies, see Hirsche and Funk, pp. 432–436.

  2. Manuscripts and editions. The number of extant MSS. is about 500. See Kentenich, p. 294. Funk, p. 420, gives 13 MSS. dated before 1500, ascribing the Imitation to à Kempis. The autograph copy, contained in the Brussels codex of 1441, has already been mentioned. It must be said, however, the conclusion reached by Hirsche, Pohl, Funk, Schulze and others that this text is autographic has been denied by Puyol and Kentenich, on the basis of its divergences from other copies, which they claim the author could not have made. A second autograph, in Louvaine (see Schulze, p. 730), seems to be nearly as old, 1420, and has the note scriptus manibus et characteribus Thomae qui est autor horum devotorum libellorum, "written by the hand of Thomas," etc. (Pohl, VI. 456 sq.). A third MS., stating that Thomas is the author, and preserved in Brussels, is dated 1425.—As for the printed editions of the fifteenth century, at least 13 present Thomas as the author, from the edition of Augsburg, 1472, to the editions of Paris, 1493, 1500.

  3. Style and contents. These agree closely with à Kempis’ other writings, and the flow of thought is altogether similar to that of his Meditation on Christ’s Incarnation. Spitzen seems to have made it at least very probable that the author was acquainted with the writings of Ruysbroeck, John of Schoenhoven, and other mystics and monks of the Lowlands. Funk has brought out references to ecclesiastical customs which fit the book into the time between the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries. Hirsche laid stress on Germanisms in the style.

Among recent German scholars, Denifle sets aside à Kempis’ claims and ascribes the work to some unknown canon regular of the Lowlands. Karl Müller, in a brief note, Kirchengesch., II. 122, and Loof’s Dogmengesch., 4th ed., p. 633, pronounce the à Kempis authorship more than doubtful. On the other hand, Schwab, Hirsche, Schulze and Funk agree that the claims of Thomas are almost beyond dispute. It is almost impossible to give a reason why the Imitation should have been ascribed to the Dutch mystic, if he were not indeed its author. The explanation given by Kentenich, p. 603, seems to be utterly insufficient.

§ 36. The German Theology.

The evangelical teachings of the little book, known as The German Theology, led Ullmann to place its author in the list of the Reformers before the Reformation.(99) The author was one of the Friends of God, and no writing issuing from that circle has had a more honorable and useful career. Together with the Imitation of Christ, it has been the most profitable of the writings of the German mystics. Its fame is derived from Luther’s high praise as much as from its own excellent contents. The Reformer issued two editions of it, 1516, with a partial text, and 1518, in the second edition giving it the name which remains with it to this day, Ein Deutsch Theologia — A German treatise of Theology.(100) Luther designated as its author a Frankfurt priest, a Teutonic knight, but for a time it was ascribed to Tauler. The Preface of the oldest MS., dated 1497, and found in 1850, made this view impossible, for Tauler is himself quoted in ch. XIII. Here the author is called a Frankfurt priest and a true Friend of God.

Luther announced his high obligation to the teachings of the manual of the way of salvation when he said that next to the Bible and St. Augustine, no book had come into his hands from which he had learnt more of what God and man and all things are and would wish to learn more. The author, he affirmed, was a pure Israelite who did not take the foam from the surface, but drew from the bed of the Jordan. Here, he continued, the teachings of the Scriptures are set forth as plain as day which have been lying under the desk of the universities, nay, have almost been left to rot in dust and muck. With his usual patriotism, he declared that in the book he had found Christ in the German tongue as he and the other German theologians had never found him in Greek, Latin or Hebrew.

The German Theology sets forth man’s sinful and helpless condition, Christ’s perfection and mediatorial work and calls upon men to have access to God through him as the door. In all its fifty-four chapters no reference is made to Mary or to the justifying nature of good works or the merit of sacramental observances.(101) It abounds as no other writing of the German mystics did in quotations from the New Testament. In its pages the wayfaring man may find the path of salvation marked out without mystification.

The book, starting out with the words of St. Paul, "when that which is perfect is come, then that which is in part shall be done away," declares that that which is imperfect has only a relative existence and that, whenever the Perfect becomes known by the creature, then "the I, the Self and the like must all be given up and done away." Christ shows us the way by having taken on him human nature. In chs. XV.-LIV., it shows that all men are dead in Adam, and that to come to the perfect life, the old man must die and the new man be born. He must become possessed with God and depossessed of the devil. Obedience is the prime requisite of the new manhood. Sin is disobedience, and the more "of Self and Me, the more of sin and wickedness and the more the Self, the I, the Me, the Mine, that is, self-seeking and selfishness, abate in a man, the more doth God’s I, that is, God Himself, increase." By obedience we become free. The life of Christ is the perfect model, and we follow him by hearkening unto his words to forsake all. This is nothing else than saying that we must be in union with the divine will and be ready either to do or to suffer. Such a man, a man who is a partaker of the divine nature, will in sincerity love all men and things, do them good and take pleasure in their welfare. Knowledge and light profit nothing without love. Love maketh a man one with God. The last word is that no man can come unto the Father but by Christ.

In 1621 the Catholic Church placed the Theologia Germanica on the Index. If all the volumes listed in that catalogue of forbidden books were like this one, making the way of salvation plain, its pages would be illuminated with ineffable light.(102)

§ 37. English Mystics.

England, in the fourteenth century, produced devotional writings which have been classed in the literature of mysticism. They are wanting in the transcendental flights of the German mystics, and are, for the most part, marked by a decided practical tendency.

The Ancren Riwle was written for three sisters who lived as anchoresses at Tarrant Kaines, Dorsetshire.(103) It was the custom in their day in England for women living a recluse life to build a room against the wall of some church or a small structure in a churchyard and in such a way that it had windows, but no doors of egress. This little book of religious counsels was written at the request of the sisters, and is usually ascribed to Simon of Ghent, bishop of Salisbury, d. 1315. The author gives two general directions, namely, to keep the heart "smooth and without any scar of evil," and to practise bodily discipline, which "serveth the first end, and of which Paul said that it profiteth little." The first is the lady, the second the handmaid. If asked to what order they belonged, the sisters were instructed to say to the Order of St. James, for James said, "Pure religion and undefiled before our God and Father is this: to visit the fatherless and widows in their affliction and to keep one’s self unspotted from the world." It is interesting to note that they are bidden to have warm clothes for bed and back, and to wash "as often as they please." They were forbidden to lash themselves with a leathern thong, or one loaded with lead except at the advice of their confessor. Richard Rolle, d. 1349, the author of a number of devotional treatises, and also translations or paraphrases of the Psalms, Job, the Canticles and Jeremiah, suddenly left Oxford, where he was pursuing his studies, discontented with the scholastic method in vogue at the university, and finally settled down as a hermit at Hampole, near Doncaster. Here he attained a high fame for piety and as a worker of miracles. He wrote in Latin and English, his chief works being the Latin treatises, The Emendation of Life and The Fervor of Love. They were translated in 1434, 1435, by Rich Misyn. His works are extant in many manuscript copies. Rolle exalted the contemplative life, indulged in much dreamy religious speculation, but also denounced the vice and worldliness of his time. In the last state of the contemplative life he represents man as "seeing into heaven with his ghostly eye."(104)

Juliana of Norwich, who died 1443, as it is said, at the age of 100, was also an anchoress, having her cell in the churchyard of St. Julian’s church, Norwich. She received 16 revelations, the first in 1373, when she was 30 years old. At that time, she saw "God in a point." She laid stress upon love, and presented the joyful aspect of religion. God revealed Himself to her in three properties, life, light and love. Her account of her revelations is pronounced by Inge "a fragrant little book."(105)

The Ladder of Perfection, written by Walter Hylton, an Augustinian canon of Thurgarton, Nottinghamshire, who died 1396,(106) depicts the different stages of spiritual attainment from the simple knowledge of the facts of religion, which is likened to the water of Cana which must be turned into wine, to the last stages of contemplation and divine union. There is no great excellency, Hylton says, "in watching and fasting till thy head aches, nor in running to Rome or Jerusalem with bare feet, nor in building churches and hospitals." But it is a sign of excellency if a man can love a sinner, while hating the sin. Those who are not content with merely saving their souls, but go on to the higher degrees of contemplation, are overcome by "a good darkness," a state in which the soul is free and not distracted by anything earthly. The light then arises little by little. Flashes come through the chinks in the walls of Jerusalem, but Jerusalem is not reached by a bound. There must be transformation, and the power that transforms is the love of God shed abroad in the soul. Love proceeds from knowledge, and the more God is known, the more is He loved. Hylton’s wide reputation is proved by the ascription of Thomas à Kempis’ Imitation to him and its identification in manuscripts with his De musica ecclesiastica.(107)

These writings, if we except Rolle, betray much of that sobriety of temper which characterizes the English religious thought. They contain no flights of hazy mystification and no rapturous outbursts of passionate feeling. They emphasize features common to all the mystics of the later Middle Ages, the gradual transformation through the power of love into the image of God, and ascent through inward contemplation to full fellowship with Him. They show that the principles of the imitation of Christ were understood on the English side of the channel as well as by the mystics of the Lowlands, and that true godliness is to be reached in another way than by the mere practice of sacramental rites.

These English pietists are to be regarded, however, as isolated figures who, so far as we know, had no influence in preparing the soil for the seed of the Reformation that was to come, as had the Pietists who lived along the Rhine.(108)

Original Footnotes

(1; original 427): See Inge, Engl. Mystics, p. 37. This author, in his Christian Mysticism, p. 5, gives the definition that mysticism is "the attempt to realize in the thought and feeling the immanence of the temporal in the eternal and of the eternal in the temporal." His statements in another place, The Inner Way, pp. xx-xxii, are more simple and illuminating. The mystical theology is that knowledge of God and of divine things which is derived not from observation or from argument but from conscious experience. The difficulty of giving a precise definition of mysticism is seen in the definitions Inge cites, Christian Mysticism, Appendix A. Comp. Deutsch, p. 632 sq

(2; original 428): It is quite in keeping with this contrast that Pfleiderer, in his Religionsphilosophie, excludes the German mystics from a place in the history of German philosophy on the ground that their thinking was not distinctly systematic. He, however, gives a brief statement to Eckart, but excludes Jacob Boehme.

(3; original 429): Dogmengesch., p. 631.

(4; original 430): Nicoll, Garden of Nuts, p. 31, says, "We study the mystics to learn from them. It need not be disguised that there are great difficulties in the way. The mystics are the most individual of writers," etc.

(5; original 431): See Preger, I. 8, and Ullmann, Reformatoren, II. 203. Harnack goes far when he denies all originality to the German mystics. Of Eckart he says, Dogmengesch. III. 378, "I give no extracts from his writings because I do not wish to seem to countenance the error that the German mystics expressed anything we cannot read in Origen, Plotinus, the Areopagite, Augustine, Erigena, Bernard and Thomas Aquinas, or that they represented a stage of religious progress." The message they announced was certainly a fresh one to their generation, even if all they said bad been said before. They spoke from the living sources of their own spiritual experience. They were not imitators. Harnack, however, goes on to give credit to the German mystics for fulfilling a mission when he says they are of invaluable worth for the history of doctrine and the church history of Germany. In the same connection he denies the distinction between mysticism and scholastic theology." Mysticism," he asserts, "cannot exist in the Protestant Church, and the Protestant who is a mystic and does not become a Roman Catholic is a dilettante." This condemnation is based upon the untenable premise that mysticism is essentially conventual, excluding sane intellectual criticism and a practical out-of-doors Christianity.

(6; original 432): Eckart’s name is written in almost every conceivable way in the documents. See Büttner, p. xxii, as Eckardus, Eccardus, Egghardus; Deutsch and Delacroix, Eckart; Pfeiffer, Preger, Inge and Langenberg, Eckhart; Denifle and Büttner, Eckehart. His writings give us scarcely a single clew to his fortunes. Quiétif-Echard was the first to lift the veil from portions of his career. See Preger, I. 325.

(7; original 433): Deutsch, Herzog, V. 149, says that parts of Eckart’s sermons might serve as models of German style to-day.

(8; original 434): Flacius Illyricus includes the second Mechthild in his Catal. veritatis. For the lives of these women and the editions of their works, see Preger, I. 71-132, and the artt. of Deutsch and Zöckler in Herzog. Some of the elder Mechthild’s predictions and descriptions seem to have been used by Dante. See Preger, p. 103 sq. Mechthild v. Magdeburg: D. fliessende Licht der Gottheit, Berlin, 1907.

Preger, I. 471 sqq., gives the Latin text of Eckart’s statement of Jan. 24, 1327, before the archiepiscopal court, his public statement of innocence in the Dominican church and the document containing the court’s refusal to allow his appeal to Rome.

(13; original 439): The 26 articles, as Denifle has shown, were based upon Eckart’s Latin writings. John’s bull is given by Preger, I. 479-482, and by Denifle, Archiv, II. 636-640. Preger, I. 365 sqq., Delacroix, p. 238 and Deutsch, V. 145, insist that Eckart made no specific recantation. The pope’s reference must have been to the statement Eckart made in the Dominican church, which contained the words, "I will amend and revoke in general and in detail, as often as may be found opportune, whatever is discovered to have a less wholesome sense, intellectum minus sane.

(14; original 440): Büttner, p. 14; Pfeiffer, p. 192, etc.

(16; original 442): p. 384.

(17; original 443): Denifle lays down the proposition that Eckart is above all a Schoolman, and that whatever there is of good in him is drawn from Thomas Aquinas. These conclusions are based upon Eckart’s Latin writings. Deutsch, V. 15, says that the form of Eckart’s thought in the Latin writings is scholastic, but the heart is mystical. Delacroix, p. 277 sqq., denies that Eckart was a scholastic and followed Thomas. Wetzer-Welte, IV. 11, deplores as Eckart’s defect that he departed from "the solid theology of Scholasticism" and took up Neo-Platonic vagaries. If Eckart had been a servile follower of Thomas, it is hard to understand how he should have laid himself open in 28 propositions to condemnation for heresy.

Loofs, p. 630, somewhat ambiguously says, Die ganze Eckehartsche Mystik ist verständlich als eine Erfassung der thomistischen und augustinischen Tradition unter dem Gesichtswinkel des Areopagiten.

(69; original 495): The extent to which Eckart influenced the mystics of the Lowlands is a matter of dispute. The clergy strove to keep his works from circulation. Langenberg, pp. 184-204, gives descriptions and excerpts from four MSS. of Eckart’s writings in Low German, copied in the convent of Nazareth, near Bredevoorde, and now preserved in the royal library of Berlin, but they do not give Eckart as the author.

Hyr na volget eyn lere schone

Teghen dantzen unde van den meybome.

Here follows a nice teaching against dancing and the May tree. One reason given against dancing was that the dancers stretched out their arms, and so showed disrespect to Christ, who stretched out his arms on the cross. One of the documents is a letter in which a monk warns his niece, who had gone astray, against displays of dress and bold gestures, intended to attract the attention of young men, especially on the Cathedral Square. With the letter he sent his niece a book of devotional literature.

Kettlewell’s statements about à Kempis’ active religious services are imaginary, I. 31, 322, etc. See Lindsay’s statement, Enc. Brit., XIV. 32.

(95; original 521): Pohl’s ed., II. 1-59; V. 1-363.

(96; original 522): De disciplina claustralium, Pohl’s ed., II. 313. For prayers to Mary III. 355-368 and sermons on Mary, VI. 218-238.

(97; original 523): Pohl, III. 357; VI. 219, 235 sq.

(98; original 524): III. 317-322.

(99; original 525): The best German ed., Stuttgart, 1858. The text is taken from Pfeiffer’s ed., Strassburg, 1851, 3d ed. unchanged; Gütersloh, 1875, containing Luther’s Preface of 1518 and the Preface of Joh. Arndt, 1632. Pfeiffer used the MS. dated 1497, the oldest in existence. The best Engl. trans., by Susannah Winkworth, from Pfeiffer’s text, London, 1854, Andover, 1856. The Andover ed. contains an Introd. by Miss Winkworth, a Letter from Chevalier Bunsen and Prefaces by Canon Kingsley and Prof. Calvin E. Stowe.

(100; original 526): Luther’s full title in the edition. of 1518 is Ein Deutsch Theologia, das ist ein edles Büchlein vom rechten Verstande was Adam und Christus sei und wie Adam in uns sterben und Christus in uns erstehen soll. A German theology, that is, a right noble little book about the right comprehension of what Adam and Christ are, and how Adam is to die in us and Christ is to arise. Cohrs in Herzog, XIX. 626, mentions 28 editions as having appeared in High German previous to 1742. Luther’s Prefaces are given in the Weimar ed. of his Works, pp. 153, 376-378.

(101; original 527): Dr. Calvin E. Stowe said "the book sets forth the essential principle of the Gospel in its naked simplicity," Winkworth’s ed., p. v.

(102; original 528): Stöckl and other Catholics, though not all, are bitter against the Theologia and charge it with pantheism. Bunsen ranked it next to the Bible. Winkworth’s ed., p. liv.

(103; original 529): The Ancren Riwle, ed. by J. Morton, Camden series, London, 1853. See W. R. Inge, Studies in Engl. Mystics, London. 1906. p. 38 sqq.

(104; original 530): C. Horstman, Richard Rolle of Hampole, 2 vols. The Early Engl. Text Soc. publ. the Engl. versions of Misyn, 1896. G. G. Perry edited his liturgy in the vol. giving the York Breviary, Surtees Soc. The poem, Pricke of Conscience, was issued by H. B. Bramley, Oxford, 1884. See Stephen, Dict. Natl. Biog. XLIX. 164-165.

(105; original 531): The Revelations of Divine Love has been ed. by R. F. S. Cressy, London, 1670, reprinted 1843; by H. Collins, London, 1817, and by Grace Warrack. 3d ed. Lond., 1909. See Inge and Dict. of Natl. Biog.

(106; original 532): Written in English, the Ladder was translated by the Carmelite friar, Thomas Fyslawe, into Latin. Hylton’s death is also put in 1433.

(107; original 533): The Ladder of Perfection was printed 1494, 1506, and has been recently ed. by R. E. Guy, London, 1869, and J. B. Dalgairns, London, 1870. See Inge, pp. 81-124; Montmorency, Thomas à Kempis, etc., pp. 138-174; and Dict. of Natl. Biog., XXVI. 435 sqq.

(108; original 534): Montmorency, p. 69, makes a remark for which, so far as I know, there is no corroborative testimony in the writings of the English Reformers, that "in this English mystical movement—of which a vast unprinted literature survives—is to be found the origin of Lollardism and of the Reformation in England."

脚注

  1. 见英奇 (Inge)，《英国神秘主义者》(Engl. Mystics)，第37页。这位作者在《基督教神秘主义》(Christian Mysticism)第5页给出的定义是，神秘主义乃“试图在思想和情感中体认暂时者内在于永恒者、永恒者内在于暂时者”。他在另一处，即《内在之路》(The Inner Way)第xx–xxii页，所作的陈述更为简明而富启发性。神秘神学是关于上帝和神圣事物的知识，并非来自观察或论证，而是来自自觉的经验。从英奇在《基督教神秘主义》附录A所引的各种定义中，可以看出，要为神秘主义下一个精确的定义何等困难。比较多伊奇 (Deutsch)，第632页及下页。↩︎

  2. 普弗莱德勒 (Pfleiderer) 在《宗教哲学》(Religionsphilosophie)中，以德国神秘主义者的思想缺乏明确的系统性为由，不让他们在德国哲学史上占有一席之地，这与上述对照颇相吻合。不过，他仍简略论及埃克哈特 (Eckart)，却将雅各布·波墨 (Jacob Boehme) 排除在外。↩︎

  3. 《教义史》(Dogmengesch.)，第631页。↩︎

  4. 尼科尔 (Nicoll) 在《核桃园》(Garden of Nuts)第31页说：“我们研究神秘主义者，是为了向他们学习。不必讳言，这条路上有极大的困难。神秘主义者是最具个性的作家”，等等。↩︎

  5. 见普雷格尔 (Preger)，第I卷，第8页；乌尔曼 (Ullmann)，《改革者》(Reformatoren)，第II卷，第203页。哈纳克 (Harnack) 否认德国神秘主义者有任何独创性，未免走得太远。他论到埃克哈特时说，见《教义史》(Dogmengesch.)第III卷，第378页：“我不摘引他的著作，因为我不愿显得在支持这样一种错误，即德国神秘主义者曾表达过任何我们在奥利金 (Origen)、普罗提诺 (Plotinus)、亚略巴古者 (the Areopagite)、奥古斯丁 (Augustine)、爱留根纳 (Erigena)、伯尔纳 (Bernard) 和托马斯·阿奎那 (Thomas Aquinas) 的著作中读不到的东西，或认为他们代表了宗教进步的一个阶段。”即使他们所说的一切从前都有人说过，他们所宣告的信息对于他们那一代人，也无疑是新鲜的。他们的话出自自身属灵经验的活泉。他们不是模仿者。不过，哈纳克接着又承认德国神秘主义者完成了一项使命，说他们对于德国的教义史和教会史具有无可估量的价值。在同一处，他否认神秘主义与经院神学之间的区别。他断言：“神秘主义不可能存在于新教教会之中；一个新教徒若是神秘主义者，却不成为罗马天主教徒，那就只是个浅尝者。”这种谴责所依据的前提不能成立：即认为神秘主义本质上属于修院生活，排斥健全的理智批判，也排斥一种走出院墙、付诸实践的基督教。↩︎

  6. 埃克哈特 (Eckart) 的姓名在文献中几乎有各种可以想象的拼法。见比特纳 (Büttner)，第xxii页，如 Eckardus、Eccardus、Egghardus；多伊奇 (Deutsch) 和德拉克鲁瓦 (Delacroix) 写作 Eckart；普法伊费尔 (Pfeiffer)、普雷格尔 (Preger)、英奇 (Inge) 和朗根贝格 (Langenberg) 写作 Eckhart；德尼夫勒 (Denifle) 和比特纳写作 Eckehart。他的著作几乎没有为我们提供任何有关其生平际遇的线索。奎蒂夫—埃沙尔 (Quiétif-Echard) 首先揭开了他部分生涯的帷幕。见普雷格尔，第I卷，第325页。↩︎

  7. 多伊奇 (Deutsch) 在赫尔佐格 (Herzog) 编著第V卷第149页说，埃克哈特 (Eckart) 的部分讲章，即使在今天也可作为德语文体的典范。↩︎

  8. 弗拉修斯·伊利里库斯 (Flacius Illyricus) 在《真理见证者目录》(Catal. veritatis)中收录了第二位梅希特希尔德 (Mechthild)。关于这些妇女的生平及其著作的各版本，见普雷格尔 (Preger)，第I卷，第71–132页，以及多伊奇 (Deutsch) 和策克勒 (Zöckler) 在赫尔佐格 (Herzog) 编著中的条目。年长的梅希特希尔德的一些预言和描写，似乎曾为但丁 (Dante) 所采用。见普雷格尔，第103页及下页。马格德堡的梅希特希尔德 (Mechthild v. Magdeburg)：《神性流溢之光》(D. fliessende Licht der Gottheit)，柏林，1907年。↩︎

  9. 《德行的七条先行准则》(Die sieben Vorregeln der Tugend)和《德行之镜》(der Spiegel der Tugend)，均由普法伊费尔 (Pfeiffer) 收录，并附有其他短论，其中一些的真伪受到怀疑。见普雷格尔 (Preger)，第I卷，第268–283页，以及莱姆普 (Lempp) 在赫尔佐格 (Herzog) 编著第IV卷第503页及下页的论述。↩︎

  10. 德尼夫勒 (Denifle)，《档案》(Archiv)等，第II卷，第240、529页。↩︎

  11. 在德尼夫勒 (Denifle) 进行考证以前，人们通常将他的出生地记为斯特拉斯堡 (Strassburg)。见德尼夫勒，第355页。↩︎

  12. Ego magister Ekardus, doctor sac. theol., protestor ante omnia, quod omnem errorem in fide et omnem deformitatem in moribus semper in quantum mihi possibile fuit, sum detestatus，等等，意为：“我，神圣神学博士埃克哈特大师，首先声明：凡信仰上的一切错误和道德上的一切败坏，我一向竭尽所能，深恶痛绝。”见普雷格尔 (Preger)，第I卷，第475–478页。普雷格尔，第I卷，第471页及后，载有埃克哈特于1327年1月24日在大主教法庭前所作陈述、他在多明我会教堂公开发表的无罪声明，以及法庭拒绝准许他向罗马上诉的文书，均为拉丁文原文。↩︎

  13. 德尼夫勒 (Denifle) 已经指出，这26条是以埃克哈特 (Eckart) 的拉丁文著作为依据的。约翰 (John) 的诏书见普雷格尔 (Preger)，第I卷，第479–482页，以及德尼夫勒，《档案》(Archiv)，第II卷，第636–640页。普雷格尔，第I卷，第365页及后，德拉克鲁瓦 (Delacroix)，第238页，以及多伊奇 (Deutsch)，第V卷，第145页，都坚持认为埃克哈特没有作过任何具体的撤回声明。教皇所指的必定是埃克哈特在多明我会教堂所作的声明，其中有这样的话：“凡被发现含义不够健全的地方，intellectum minus sane，我都会在适当的时候，从整体到细节加以修正并撤回。”↩︎

  14. 比特纳 (Büttner)，第14页；普法伊费尔 (Pfeiffer)，第192页等。↩︎

  15. 普法伊费尔 (Pfeiffer)，第216页。↩︎

  16. 第384页。↩︎

  17. 德尼夫勒 (Denifle) 提出，埃克哈特 (Eckart) 首先是一位经院学者，而他思想中凡属良善的内容，都是从托马斯·阿奎那 (Thomas Aquinas) 那里汲取的。这些结论是依据埃克哈特的拉丁文著作得出的。多伊奇 (Deutsch)，第V卷，第15页说，埃克哈特在拉丁文著作中的思想，形式是经院式的，内核却是神秘主义的。德拉克鲁瓦 (Delacroix)，第277页及后，否认埃克哈特是经院学者，也否认他追随托马斯。《韦策尔—韦尔特百科全书》(Wetzer-Welte)，第IV卷，第11页，惋惜地指出，埃克哈特的缺点在于偏离了“经院哲学坚实的神学”，转而接受新柏拉图主义的奇想。倘若埃克哈特只是托马斯亦步亦趋的追随者，就很难理解，他何以竟有28条命题使自己招致异端的定罪。↩︎

  18. 哈纳克 (Harnack) 认为，埃克哈特 (Eckart) 的神学不过是爱留根纳 (Erigena)、狄奥尼修斯 (Dionysius) 和普罗提诺 (Plotinus) 思想的再现；德拉克鲁瓦 (Delacroix) 和洛夫斯 (Loofs) 也持类似看法，只是有所保留。德拉克鲁瓦在第240页说：sur tous les points essentiels, il est d’accord avec Plotin et Proclus，即“在一切要点上，他都与普罗提诺和普罗克洛 (Proclus) 一致”。但在另一处，第260页，他又说，埃克哈特从新柏拉图主义汲取了若干主要观念，并“加以发挥、改造和转化”。洛夫斯在第630页以颇为含混的措辞说：Die ganze Eckehartsche Mystik ist verständlich als eine Erfassung der thomistischen und augustinischen Tradition unter dem Gesichtswinkel des Areopagiten. 即：“埃克哈特的全部神秘主义，都可以理解为从亚略巴古者 (the Areopagite) 的视角对托马斯主义和奥古斯丁传统的把握。”↩︎

  19. 普法伊费尔 (Pfeiffer)，第254、540页。↩︎

  20. 普法伊费尔 (Pfeiffer)，第268页。下述一页可见埃克哈特 (Eckart) 下定义时何等深奥难解。他说，上帝的 einveltigin Natur ist von Formen formelos, von Werdenen werdelos, von Wesenen weselos und ist von Sachen sachelos，即“单纯本性，相对于诸形式而无形式，相对于诸生成而无生成，相对于诸存在而无存在，相对于诸事物而无事物”。普法伊费尔，第497页。↩︎

  21. 普法伊费尔 (Pfeiffer)，第282、311、579页。↩︎

  22. In dem Vater sind Bilde allerCreaturen，即“父里面有一切受造物的原型”。普法伊费尔 (Pfeiffer)，第269、285页等。↩︎

  23. Die Seele in ihrem Grunde ist so unsprechlich als Gott unsprechlich ist，即“灵魂在其根基之中，与上帝一样不可言说”。普法伊费尔 (Pfeiffer)，第89页。↩︎

  24. 第39、113、193、286页等。普弗莱德勒 (Pfleiderer)，第6页，称之为灵魂的灵——der Geist der Seele；多伊奇 (Deutsch)，第152页，则称之为“灵魂最内在的根基”——der innerst Seelengrund。↩︎

  25. 第113、152、286、487、530页。↩︎

  26. 《灵魂的高贵》(Die Edelkeit der Seele)、《论灵魂的尊严》(Von der Würdgkeit der Seele)、《论灵魂的高贵》(Von dem Adel der Seele)。普法伊费尔 (Pfeiffer)，第382–448页。↩︎

  31. Hier ist Gottes Grund mein Grund und mein Grund Gottes Grund. Hier lebe ich aus meinem Eigenen, wie Gott aus seinem Eigenen lebt，即：“在这里，上帝的根基就是我的根基，我的根基就是上帝的根基。在这里，我本乎自己而活，如同上帝本乎他自己而活。”比特纳 (Büttner)，第100页。↩︎

  32. Lautere, alles Erschaffenen ledige Abgeschiedenheit，即“纯粹的、摆脱一切受造之物的超脱”。这篇讲章见比特纳 (Büttner)，第9页及后。↩︎

  35. 普法伊费尔 (Pfeiffer)，第II卷，第546、564、633页：Niht endienent unserin were dar zuo dass uns Got iht gebe oder tuo. 即：“我们的行为并非为使上帝赐给我们什么，或为我们做什么。”↩︎

  36. Es geht ein Geist evangelischer Freiheit durch Eckart’s Sittenlehre welcher zugleich ein Geist der Freudigkeit ist，即：“埃克哈特的伦理教导贯穿着一种福音自由的精神，同时也是一种喜乐的精神。”普雷格尔 (Preger)，第I卷，第452页。见论马利亚的讲章，普法伊费尔 (Pfeiffer)，第47–53页；另见第18–21、607页。↩︎

  37. Das Auge das da inne ich Gott sehe, das ist selbe Auge da inne mich Gott sieht. Mein Auge und Gottes Auge, das ist ein Auge, und ein Erkennen und ein Gesicht und ein Minnen，即：“我借以看见上帝的眼睛，就是上帝借以看见我的同一只眼睛。我的眼睛与上帝的眼睛，是同一只眼睛、同一认识、同一观看、同一爱。”普法伊费尔 (Pfeiffer)，第312页。↩︎

  38. 拉松 (Lasson) 在于伯韦格 (Ueberweg) 著作第I卷第471页对此有很好的表述。英奇 (Inge) 在第150页说，埃克哈特 (Eckart) 坦荡的诚实、强大的思想力量，加上深切的虔敬与纯洁的心灵，使他成为基督教哲学史上最引人注目的人物之一。↩︎

  39. 普法伊费尔 (Pfeiffer)，第II卷，第155、390页。↩︎

  40. 第7页。普雷格尔 (Preger) 在结束对埃克哈特 (Eckart) 的论述时说，第I卷，第458页，正是他真正奠定了基督教哲学的基础。Er erst hat die christliche Philosophie eigentlich begründet.↩︎

  41. 普雷格尔 (Preger)，第III卷，第131页。最古老的斯特拉斯堡 (Strassburg) 手稿称陶勒 (Tauler) 为 erluhtete begnodete Lerer，即“蒙光照、蒙恩典的教师”。见施密特 (Schmidt)，第159页。普雷格尔，第III卷，第93页，列出了若干居住在斯特拉斯堡、姓 Taweler 或 Tawler 的人名。↩︎

  42. 克里斯蒂娜 (Christina) 写过一部题为《恩典之满溢》(Von der Gnaden Ueberlast)的书，叙述她所在修院中姊妹们所过的紧张生活。她宣称，圣灵拨动陶勒 (Tauler) 的心，如同弹奏鲁特琴；她又曾在异象中得到启示，知道他那炽热的舌头将使大地燃烧起来。见施特劳赫 (Strauch) 在赫尔佐格 (Herzog) 编著第V卷第129页及下页的条目。另见普雷格尔 (Preger)，第II卷，第247–251、277页及后。↩︎

  43. 斯特拉斯堡 (Strassburg) 编年史家施佩克林 (Specklin) 说，陶勒 (Tauler) 讲话“声调清晰，热情真挚。他的目的是使人感到世界的虚无。他既谴责教士，也谴责平信徒”。↩︎

  44. 温克沃思小姐 (Miss Winkworth) 在第1–73页提供了此书的译文。书中称陶勒的劝诫者为 der grosse Gottesfreund im Oberlande，即“上地的伟大上帝之友”。见§ 32。↩︎

  45. 其中一篇讲章以《约翰福音》16:7–11为经文，阐明圣灵的影响，黑尔会吏长 (Archdeacon Hare) 在《保惠师的使命》(Mission of the Comforter)中曾大段引用。另见温克沃思小姐 (Miss Winkworth)，第350、358页。↩︎

  48. 《内在之路》(Inner Way)，第200页。温克沃思小姐 (Miss Winkworth)，第345、360页及后。↩︎

  49. 普雷格尔 (Preger)，第III卷，第132页；温克沃思小姐 (Miss Winkworth)，第348页。↩︎

  50. 普雷格尔 (Preger)，第III卷，第131页；温克沃思小姐 (Miss Winkworth)，第355页。↩︎

  51. 克斯特林 (Köstlin)，《马丁·路德传》(Life of M. Luther)，第I卷，第117页及下页、第126页。梅兰希通 (Melanchthon) 在陶勒 (Tauler) 著作法兰克福版 (Franf. ed.) 的序言中说：“在近代作者中，陶勒无疑首屈一指。然而，我听说，有些人竟敢否认这位备受尊崇之人的基督教教导。”贝扎 (Beza) 却持不同看法，称陶勒为幻想家。见施密特 (Schmidt)，第160页。普雷格尔 (Preger)，第III卷，第194页，甚至说陶勒明确教导了福音派的称义教义。↩︎

  52. 《内在之路》(The Inner Way)，第57页及后、第77页及后。↩︎

  53. 比尔迈尔 (Bihlmeyer)，第65页，判定苏索 (Suso) 的出生年份很可能是1295年。其他作者则将其推迟至1300年。↩︎

  54. 全书共53章。迪彭布罗克 (Diepenbrock) 版，第137–306页；比尔迈尔 (Bihlmeyer) 版，第1–195页。迪彭布罗克版已转写为现代德语，对现代读者较为便利。↩︎

  55. 这些定义的译文，见英奇 (Inge)，《光、生命与爱》(Light,Life and Love)，第66–82页。↩︎

  56. 苏索 (Suso) 用拉丁文修订了自己的著作，题为《永恒智慧之钟》(Horologium eternoe sapientiae)，陶勒 (Tauler) 似乎曾拥有此书的一部抄本。普雷格尔 (Preger)，第II卷，第324页。↩︎

  57. 比尔迈尔 (Bihlmeyer) 版，第13页。↩︎

  58. 《自传》(Autobiog.)，第XIV章，比尔迈尔 (Bihlmeyer) 版，第38页。↩︎

  59. 《论永恒的智慧》(Von der ewigen Weisheit)，比尔迈尔 (Bihlmeyer) 版，第296页及下页。↩︎

  60. 普雷格尔 (Preger)，第III卷，第370页；施特劳赫 (Strauch)，第205页。↩︎

  61. 见鲁尔曼·梅尔斯温 (Rulman Merswin) 在《九岩》(Nine Rocks)第XIII、XIV章中对贝居安姊妹 (Beguines) 和贝格哈德弟兄 (Beghards) 的谴责。↩︎

  62. 据普雷格尔 (Preger)，第III卷，第417页及下页所刊文本。↩︎

  63. 见R. 梅尔斯温 (R. Merswin)《九岩》(Nine Rocks)的末章。↩︎

  64. 最主要的两部著作是《两人之书》(Das Buch ron den zwei Mannen)，记述作者归信后最初五年的经历，载于施密特 (Schmidt) 的《巴塞尔的尼古拉》(Nic. von Basel)，第205–277页；以及《五人之书》(Das Buch von den fünf Mannen)，上地人在其中记述了自己和友人的生平。完整的著作目录，见普雷格尔 (Preger)，第III卷，第270页及后，以及施特劳赫 (Strauch)，第209页及后。↩︎

  65. 见普雷格尔 (Preger)，第III卷，第349页及后。C. 施密特 (C. Schmidt) 载有其文本，迪彭布罗克 (Diepenbrock) 的《H. 苏索》(H Suso)，第505–572页，亦有收录。↩︎

  66. 施特劳赫 (Strauch)，第208页，及其他学者认为，梅尔斯温 (Merswin) 的著作大半是从陶勒 (Tauler) 及其他作者的作品中编纂而成。施特劳赫评其内容为絮叨——geschwätzig。《九岩》(Nine Rocks)过去常与苏索 (Suso) 的著作合刊。施密特 (Schmidt) 确定了梅尔斯温的作者身份。↩︎

  67. Rulman hat den Gottesfreund einfach erfunden，即“鲁尔曼完全是凭空杜撰了这位上帝之友”。施特劳赫 (Strauch)，第217页。↩︎

  68. 普雷格尔 (Preger) 和施密特 (Schmidt) 是主张上地人确有其人的主要代言者。里德尔 (Rieder) 最近洗去了鲁尔曼 (Rulman) 伪造的污名，将责任归于鲁汶的尼古拉 (Nicolas of Löwen)，后者于1366年进入“绿岛”(das grüne Wört)。这里着重提出了古文字学方面的考量，即尼古拉的笔迹与据称出自上地人之手的字迹相似。↩︎

  69. 埃克哈特 (Eckart) 对低地地区 (Lowlands) 的神秘主义者究竟有多大影响，仍有争议。教士们力图阻止他的著作流传。朗根贝格 (Langenberg)，第181页，引用了卒于1398年的聚特芬的格尔赫德·策尔博尔德 (Gerherd Zerbold von Zütphen) 所著《论德语书籍》(De libris Teutonicalibus)，其中主张，虽然有益的书可以用俗语阅读，但埃克哈特的著作与讲章极其有害，平信徒不得阅读。朗根贝格，第184–204页，对埃克哈特著作的四部低地德语手稿作了介绍并摘录其内容。这些手稿抄写于布雷德福尔德 (Bredevoorde) 附近的拿撒勒修院 (convent of Nazareth)，现藏于柏林皇家图书馆，但并未署名埃克哈特为作者。↩︎

  70. 恩格尔哈特 (Engelhardt)，第265–297页，对这场争论作了详尽叙述。热尔松 (Gerson) 致巴多罗买 (Bartholomew) 和舍恩霍芬 (Schoenhofen) 的信，以及舍恩霍芬的信，见杜潘 (Du Pin) 编《热尔松著作集》(Works of Gerson)，第29–82页。梅特林克 (Maeterlinck)，第4页，提到了吕斯布鲁克 (Ruysbroeck) 著作中某些段落给诠释者带来的困难。↩︎

  71. 我采用的是兰伯特 (Lambert) 第3–160页所载的德文文本。《光、生命与爱》(Light, Life and Love)中有译得很好的英文选段。↩︎

  72. 见兰伯特 (Lambert)，第62、63页等。↩︎

  73. 引自加勒 (Galle)，第184–224页。↩︎

  74. 见格鲁贝 (Grube)，《格哈德·格鲁特》(Gerh. Groot)，第9页；朗根贝格 (Langenberg)，第ix页；帕斯托尔 (Pastor)，第I卷，第150页。该弟兄团体的拉丁文名称有 fratres vitae communis（共同生活弟兄）、fratres modernae devotionis（现代虔敬弟兄）、fratres bonae voluntatis（善意弟兄），后者与《路加福音》11:14有关；又因其讲道的惯例而称为 fratres collationari（讲论弟兄）。格鲁特 (Groote) 的姓名有 Geert de Groote、Gherd de Groet（朗根贝格，第3页）、Gerhard Groot（格鲁贝）等拼法。↩︎

  75. 他因捍卫正统教导，而获得“异端之锤”——malleus hereticorum——的称号。关于这一用语的应用，见汉森 (Hansen)，《巫术迷狂史》(Gesch. des Hexenwahns)，第361页。关于格鲁特 (Groote) 作为传道人的声誉，见格鲁贝 (Grube)，第14页及后、第23页。托马斯·阿·肯皮斯 (Thomas à Kempis) 证实格鲁特作为传道人深受欢迎。见凯特尔韦尔 (Kettlewell)，第I卷，第130–134页。他已刊行的讲章中，有一篇反对教士纳妾——de focaristis。已印行讲论的目录，见赫尔佐格 (Herzog) 编著，第VII卷，第692页及后，以及朗根贝格 (Langenberg)，第35页及后。↩︎

  76. 见格鲁贝 (Grube)，第88页，以及舒尔策 (Schulze)，第492页及后；后者简要叙述了德国18所会院和低地地区 (Lowlands) 20所会院的历史。最后一所于1505年设立在康布雷 (Cambray)。↩︎

  77. 托马斯·阿·肯皮斯 (Thomas à Kempis) 在《弗洛伦提乌斯传》(Vita Florentii) 第XIV章论及拉德温 (Radewyn) 时说，劳作最有益于灵性的长进，也适于约束肉体的情欲。有一位弟兄死后被发现藏有一些钱，因而在安葬时未获准为他祈祷。↩︎

  78. 乌尔霍恩 (Uhlhorn)，第373页，举出一位这样的反对者，即代芬特尔 (Deventer) 一个名叫凯特尔 (Ketel) 的人。另见朗根贝格 (Langenberg)，第x页。↩︎

  79. 见施密德 (Schmid)，《从起初至今的教育史》(Gesch. d. Erziehung vom Anfang his auf unsere Zeit)，斯图加特 (Stuttgart)，1892年，第II卷，第164–167页；希尔舍 (Hirsche)，载赫尔佐格 (Herzog) 编著，第II卷，第759页；帕斯托尔 (Pastor) 的高度赞誉，第I卷，第152页；以及朗根贝格 (Langenberg)，第ix页。↩︎

  80. 凯特尔韦尔 (Kettlewell)，第I卷，第111页。↩︎

  81. 托马斯·阿·肯皮斯 (Thos. à Kempis)，《格哈德传》(Vita Gerard.)，第XVIII章，第11节；凯特尔韦尔 (Kettlewell)，第I卷，第166页。他宣称，教士的生活就是百姓的福音——vita clerici evangelium populi。↩︎

  82. 见朗根贝格 (Langenberg)，第51页。↩︎

  83. 见乌尔曼 (Ullman)，第II卷，第82、115页及下页。舒尔策 (Schulze)，第190页，在这一点上说得不那么清楚。凯特尔韦尔 (Kettlewell)，第II卷，第440页，说弟兄们是“为宗教改革开辟道路的主要力量”。↩︎

  84. 见朗根贝格 (Langenberg)。他所载反对舞蹈的诗，第68页及后，开篇如下——

    Hyr na volget eyn lere schone

    Teghen dantzen unde van den meybome.

    以下是一番反对舞蹈和五月树的美好教训。反对舞蹈的一个理由是，舞者伸展双臂，因而对那在十字架上伸展双臂的基督表示不敬。其中一份文献是一封信，一位修士在信中告诫他那误入歧途的侄女，不要炫耀衣饰，也不要做出大胆的姿态来吸引青年男子的注意，尤其不要在主教座堂广场上如此。他随信寄给侄女一本灵修书。↩︎

  85. 范德哈特 (Van der Hardt)，《康斯坦茨会议》(Conc. Const.)，第III卷，第107–121页，载有格拉邦 (Grabon) 的指控、达伊 (D’Ailly) 和热尔松 (Gerson) 的裁断，以及格拉邦撤回其主张的声明全文。↩︎

  86. 德韦特 (De Wette)，《路德书信集》(Luther’s Letters)，第1448、1449号，第IV卷，第358页及后。↩︎

  87. 《托马斯·阿·肯皮斯的处世智慧》(The Worldly Wisdom of Thos. à Kempis) 一文，载《都柏林评论》(Dublin Review)，1908年，第262–287页。↩︎

  88. 《系统神学》(System. Theol.)，第I卷，第79页。关于格莱斯顿 (Gladstone) 的评价，见莫利 (Morley)，第II卷，第186页。巴特勒 (Butler)，第191页，列出1502–1900年间的33种英译本。德昆西 (De Quincey) 说：“这本书的出现，回应了基督教欧洲对天上之光的叹息渴求。除新教地区的《圣经》外，人类所知的书中，没有一本享有同样的殊荣。这是有记载以来最奇妙的圣经性事实。”引自凯特尔韦尔 (Kettlewell)，第I卷。↩︎

  89. 巴克尔 (Backer) 在其《书目论考》(Essai bibliogr.) 中，列举了545种拉丁文版本和约900种法文版本。属于15世纪的版本有50余种。见丰克 (Funk)，第426页。比林根 (Bullingen) 藏书于1838年捐赠给科隆 (Cologne) 市图书馆，捐赠时包含400种不同版本。蒙莫朗西 (Montmorenci)，第xxii页及下页，列出1471–1503年间29种版本的出版日期及出版地点。↩︎

  90. 高乃依 (Corneille) 于1651年作成法文诗体译本。1837年，苏尔茨巴赫 (Sulzbach) 出版了一种多语种版本，收有拉丁文原文，以及意大利文、法文、德文、希腊文和英文译本。↩︎

  91. 希尔舍 (Hirsche) 发现了其中的韵律，并于1874年将其公之于世。↩︎

  92. 这是米尔曼 (Milman) 的评价。《拉丁基督教史》(Hist. of Lat. Christ.)，第XIV编，第3章。米尔曼说：“本书唯一、单一、排他的目的，就是净化、提升个人的灵魂——那完全与同类隔绝、独自居于自己思想领地之中的人的灵魂。”↩︎

  93. Mirum est, si non lugeat

    Experimento qui probat

    Quod vivere in soeculo

    Labor, dolor, afflictio.

    一个人若亲身体验到，活在世上就是劳苦、悲痛、患难，却仍不哀哭，那才真是奇事。

    布卢默 (Blume) 与德雷韦斯 (Dreves)：《圣诗汇编》(Analecta hymnica)，第XLVIII卷，第503页。托马斯·阿·肯皮斯 (Thomas à Kempis) 的圣诗载于布卢默与德雷韦斯，第XLVIII卷，第475–514页。↩︎

  94. In omnibus requiem quaesivi et non inveni nisi in een huechsken met een buexken。我在万事中寻求安息，却一无所获，唯有手持小书、安坐一隅时才寻得。弗朗西斯库斯·托伦西斯 (Franciscus Tolensis) 最先认定这幅画像画的是阿·肯皮斯 (à Kempis)。凯特尔韦尔 (Kettlewell) 关于阿·肯皮斯积极从事宗教服事的叙述，乃出于想象，见第I卷，第31、322页等。参见林赛 (Lindsay) 的论述，《不列颠百科全书》(Enc. Brit.)，第XIV卷，第32页。↩︎

  95. 波尔 (Pohl) 编本，第II卷，第1–59页；第V卷，第1–363页。↩︎

  96. 《修院生活的纪律》(De disciplina claustralium)，波尔 (Pohl) 编本，第II卷，第313页。向马利亚所作的祷告，见第III卷，第355–368页；论马利亚的讲章，见第VI卷，第218–238页。↩︎

  97. 波尔 (Pohl)，第III卷，第357页；第VI卷，第219、235页及下页。↩︎

  98. 第III卷，第317–322页。↩︎

  99. 最佳德文版本，斯图加特 (Stuttgart)，1858年。正文取自普法伊费尔 (Pfeiffer) 编本，斯特拉斯堡 (Strassburg)，1851年；未经改动的第3版，居特斯洛 (Gütersloh)，1875年，收有路德1518年的序言和约翰·阿恩特 (Joh. Arndt) 1632年的序言。普法伊费尔所用的是标有1497年日期的抄本，为现存最古老的抄本。最佳英译本由苏珊娜·温克沃思 (Susannah Winkworth) 根据普法伊费尔的文本译出，伦敦，1854年；安多弗 (Andover)，1856年。安多弗版收有温克沃思小姐的导论、邦森爵士 (Chevalier Bunsen) 的一封信，以及金斯利法政牧师 (Canon Kingsley) 和加尔文·E. 斯托教授 (Prof. Calvin E. Stowe) 的序言。↩︎

  100. 路德1518年版本的完整书名是 Ein Deutsch Theologia, das ist ein edles Büchlein vom rechten Verstande was Adam und Christus sei und wie Adam in uns sterben und Christus in uns erstehen soll，即《德意志神学：一本极其高贵的小书，论正确理解亚当和基督是什么，以及亚当应当如何在我们里面死去，基督应当如何在我们里面兴起》。科尔斯 (Cohrs)，载赫尔佐格 (Herzog) 编著，第XIX卷，第626页，提到1742年以前已出版28种高地德语版本。路德的序言见魏玛版《路德全集》，第153、376–378页。↩︎

  101. 加尔文·E. 斯托博士 (Dr. Calvin E. Stowe) 说：“本书以毫无修饰的质朴，阐明了福音的根本原则。”温克沃思 (Winkworth) 编本，第v页。↩︎

  102. 施特克尔 (Stöckl) 和其他一些天主教徒——但并非所有天主教徒——猛烈反对《神学》(Theologia)，指责它是泛神论。邦森 (Bunsen) 将其地位列为仅次于《圣经》。温克沃思 (Winkworth) 编本，第liv页。↩︎

  103. 《女隐修者规条》(Ancren Riwle)，J. 莫顿 (J. Morton) 编，卡姆登丛书 (Camden series)，伦敦，1853年。见W. R. 英奇 (W. R. Inge)，《英国神秘主义者研究》(Studies in Engl. Mystics)，伦敦，1906年，第38页及后。↩︎

  104. C. 霍斯特曼 (C. Horstman)，《汉波尔的理查德·罗尔》(Richard Rolle of Hampole)，2卷。早期英语文本学会 (The Early Engl. Text Soc.) 于1896年出版了米辛 (Misyn) 的英译本。G. G. 佩里 (G. G. Perry) 在收录《约克日课经》的那一卷中，编刊了他的礼仪文本，由瑟蒂斯学会 (Surtees Soc.) 出版。诗作《良心之刺》(Pricke of Conscience) 由H. B. 布拉姆利 (H. B. Bramley) 刊行，牛津，1884年。见斯蒂芬 (Stephen)，《英国人物传记辞典》(Dict. Natl. Biog.)，第XLIX卷，第164–165页。↩︎

  105. 《神圣之爱的启示》(The Revelations of Divine Love) 曾由R. F. S. 克雷西 (R. F. S. Cressy) 编刊，伦敦，1670年，1843年重印；另有H. 柯林斯 (H. Collins) 编本，伦敦，1817年，以及格雷斯·沃拉克 (Grace Warrack) 编本，第3版，伦敦，1909年。见英奇 (Inge) 及《英国人物传记辞典》(Dict. of Natl. Biog.)。↩︎

  106. 《阶梯》(Ladder) 以英文写成，由加尔默罗会修士托马斯·菲斯劳 (Thomas Fyslawe) 译为拉丁文。希尔顿 (Hylton) 的卒年也有定为1433年的。↩︎

  107. 《通往完全的阶梯》(The Ladder of Perfection) 曾于1494、1506年印行，近来又有R. E. 盖伊 (R. E. Guy) 编本，伦敦，1869年，以及J. B. 达尔盖恩斯 (J. B. Dalgairns) 编本，伦敦，1870年。见英奇 (Inge)，第81–124页；蒙莫朗西 (Montmorency)，《托马斯·阿·肯皮斯》(Thomas à Kempis) 等，第138–174页；以及《英国人物传记辞典》(Dict. of Natl. Biog.)，第XXVI卷，第435页及后。↩︎

  108. 蒙莫朗西 (Montmorency)，第69页，说：“罗拉德派运动 (Lollardism) 和英国宗教改革的起源，要到这场英国神秘主义运动中去寻找——这场运动留下了大量尚未刊印的文献。”据我所知，这一说法在英国宗教改革者的著作中并无佐证。↩︎
